The bounded initial count supplies the initial bounds. Choose ε(k − 1) < 1; induction then
bounds every Di . For i ≥ j, either the buffer of Xi misses the entire tilt, or dropping at most
k decreasing increments reduces to that situation. The already bounded ratios pay for these
increments and prove (7.5) at every fixed rate.

Weights above one. Now suppose b > 1, and write L = log b. Given a desired rate, choose
B > kL at least that large. We induct on j that

                                   Eνj eBXi ≤ C∗       (i ≥ j).

If i ≥ j + k, the tilt and buffer are disjoint, and the conditional passage estimate applies
directly. Otherwise put j0 = i − k < j, treating levels below the initial one as no tilt and
paying a fixed factor for the bounded initial count. Because Fj (b)/Fj0 (b) ≥ 1,
                                                                         
                                                                  X
                          Eνj eBXi ≤ Eνj0 exp BXi + L                  Xℓ  .
                                                              j0 <ℓ≤j

There are at most k summands in the sum. Give each its Hölder weight L/B. Its resulting
moment is Eνj0 eBXℓ ≤ C∗ , by the induction hypothesis at the earlier tilt j0 . The remaining
Hölder weight is at least 1 − kL/B > 0. Its factor is a moment of Xi at a larger fixed rate,
under a tilt whose support misses the buffer of Xi . Consequently
                                                      kL/B
                                      Eνj eBXi ≤ CC∗          .

Since kL/B < 1, a sufficiently large fixed C∗ closes the induction. This proves every desired
rate. The ratio bound follows from

                       1 ≤ Fj (b)/Fj−1 (b) = Eνj−1 bNj −Nj−1 ≤ Eνj−1 eLXj .

The preceding proofs are conditional and remain valid after adjoining finitely many passage
counts at the indicated levels.

                                               105
Moving the inner cutoff. For the reverse direction, lower the inner cutoff while keeping
the outer one fixed, counting nests whose entire raw traces lie outside the moving cutoff. Every
newly included nest supplies a passage near that cutoff by the same radial or sector-crossing
argument used in (7.3). A fixed number of reversed levels separates its buffer from the earlier,
farther-out tilt. The two inductions therefore apply verbatim with the radial order reversed.

Corollary 7.5 (Polynomial costs and fixed cutoffs). Up to radii O(nC ) in mesh units, every
fixed positive nest weight and each of its fixed real powers has expectation between n−A /C ′ and
C ′ nA , for constants depending on the fixed weight and geometry. Changing a cutoff by a fixed
factor changes its expectation by a bounded factor. Changing a permitted exterior completion
beyond such a fixed buffer, while keeping the patch, buffer, and deterministic wall portions
unchanged, does the same. For a fixed weight W , uniformly for t in a fixed compact interval,

                          log EW t+ε − log EW t ≤ C|ε| log n + O(|ε|)

when ε stays in a sufficiently small fixed interval.
Proof. There are O(log n) dyadic levels, so (7.4) proves the polynomial bounds and the fixed-
factor cutoff assertion. The bounded reciprocal marginal-density comparisons in Lemmas 6.13
and 6.16 give the exterior assertion. Finally t 7→ log EW t is convex. Its values on a slightly
larger compact interval are O(log n), by the bounds just proved; its secant slopes on the smaller
interval are therefore O(log n).

Lemma 7.6 (Absorption of macroscopic passage errors). Suppose Yn ≥ 0, EYn ≥ C −1 n−A ,
and EYn2 ≤ CnA . Suppose Xn ≥ 0 has uniformly bounded exponential moments at every fixed
rate. For fixed C0 ≥ 1 and every ε > 0,
                                             E[Yn C0±Xn ]
                                Cε−1 n−ε ≤                ≤ Cε nε .
                                                EYn
Proof. Put L = log C0 and choose η > 0 with ηL < ε/2; the case L = 0 is immediate. On
Xn ≤ η log n, either multiplier lies between n−ε/2 and nε/2 . For every fixed t > 0, Cauchy–
Schwarz gives
                                                                      1/2
           E[Yn eLXn ; Xn > η log n] ≤ (EYn2 )1/2 n−tη Ee(2L+t)Xn            ≤ Ct nA/2−tη/2 .
The same bound, with L = 0, controls the omitted E[Yn ; Xn > η log n]. Choose t so large that
both errors, after division by EYn , are smaller than any prescribed inverse power. Retaining
the truncated event proves the lower bounds, and adding the tail proves the upper bounds.
Enlarge constants for bounded n.

7.3   The strict cost at a flat wire change
Consider a straight boundary with one change of wire color, a marked gap at that change, and
a fixed buffered half-box. The gap is the boundary incidence of its designated physical tile
corner between neighboring wall ports. Its own color is the primal or dual color of that corner,
independently of the wire designations on the two adjacent wall intervals. Distinct incidences
at a cut remain distinct, and no wall cap is used to extend an actual component. Let An be
the event that the marked gap has an actual arm of its own color to radius comparable to n in
mesh units. Let Nn count raw arcs straddling the gap, with one endpoint on each side ray,
whose entire traces are inside that cutoff. Define
                                                                       √
                    P (n) = log P(An ),     D∗ (n) = log EdNn ,    d = q.                  (7.7)
Here 0 < d < 1, so no nonnegative sign is asserted for D∗ . All definitions use a fixed compatible
positive exterior completion beyond the buffer.

                                               106
Lemma 7.7 (Flat costs, gap arms, and raw straddles). There are fixed 0 < c < C < ∞ such
that, with harmless fixed changes of the cutoff convention,

                                    ACn ⊆ {Nn = 0} ⊆ Acn .                                  (7.8)

Arm probabilities at fixed multiples of a radius are comparable. Moreover

                          −A log n − O(1) ≤ P (n) ≤ −a log n + O(1)

for some A, a > 0, and
                                 −A log n − O(1) ≤ D∗ (n) ≤ 0.
Changing a cutoff by a fixed factor or changing a compatible exterior completion beyond a
fixed buffer changes either P (n) or D∗ (n) by O(1). These statements are uniform under the
compatible exterior changes allowed above.

Proof. Work in the cut drawing without using exterior caps or wire identifications in its
connectivity test; the sampling law retains its prescribed boundary partition. Within each
tile, the corner sites on a given side of a switch strand are equal or joined by an actual open
diagonal. Concatenating these local incidences identifies boundary-touching regions of the
drawing with the actual color components incident to the boundary gaps. This identification
uses only physical edges. The disjoint raw boundary-to-boundary arcs have a tree as their
region-adjacency graph. A closed raw loop has no wall port and encloses no boundary gap; it
therefore does not split a boundary-touching class. Apparent bank contacts in an unsmoothed
drawing retain their distinct fan incidences on the cut surface and do not join such classes.
     Let S be the set of tree vertices visited by the outer-rim walk. It is a connected subtree;
neither that walk nor the wall walk is assumed to be a simple path. If the region of the marked
gap is absent from S, take the first edge separating it from S. An arc with an endpoint on the
rim has both adjacent regions visited by the rim walk, so this separating edge cannot have
such an endpoint. Both ends of its raw arc are on the wall. The wall walk enters and leaves
the component containing the gap across that edge, hence the two ends lie on opposite sides
of the gap. This is a straddling raw arc. Conversely, a wall-to-wall raw arc straddling the gap
separates it from a sufficiently distant rim, and an actual path of the gap color cannot cross it.
The region argument is unchanged by interior closed loops or mixed wire designations, since
no exterior identification has been used. Applying it in two nested buffered half-boxes, with
fixed margins to absorb the tile diameter, proves (7.8).
     For a fixed-factor extension use the single-arm version of Lemma 6.18. Its finitely many
truncated-search success events T1 , . . . , Tm cover the shorter-arm event. On each successful
leaf, Proposition 6.14 and Lemma 6.11 extend the measured source-connected frontier through
the remaining window with probability at least c0 . Thus, for the permitted states F fixed
away from that window,
                                                             c0
                         P(ACn | F ) ≥ c0 max P(Ti | F ) ≥      P(An | F ).
                                           i                 m
This is a relative event estimate, not an assertion under arbitrary conditioning on the full
states of an incoming arm. Iterating from a bounded-mesh event of positive finite-energy
probability gives the polynomial lower bound. In the other direction, Proposition 6.15 provides
opposite-color actual wall-to-wall screens in separated annuli, each with uniformly positive
conditional probability. Each screen blocks the arm. Conditional iteration over O(log n) annuli
gives the strict polynomial upper bound. The bounded collar comparisons in Lemmas 6.13
and 6.16 make these estimates uniform under the specified exterior changes. None of these
paths traverses a wire identification. The assertions for D∗ follow from Corollary 7.5; its upper
bound is immediate from d < 1 and Nn ≥ 0.

                                               107
Lemma 7.8 (Strict flat-flip weight gap). For every fixed 0 < b1 < b2 , there is c > 0 such that

                             log EbN         Nn
                                   2 − log Eb1 ≥ c log n − O(1).
                                     n
                                                                                            (7.9)

In particular, for some c′ > 0,

                                  D∗ (n) ≥ P (n) + c′ log n − O(1).                        (7.10)

Proof. Fix b = b1 . At a dyadic scale ri well between the mesh and the cutoff, choose a fixed
large integer k, larger than all buffer ranges in Proposition 7.4. Let Nin count straddles wholly
inside ri−k , and let Nout count straddles whose entire raw traces lie outside ri+k and inside
the large cutoff. In particular, outside refers to the trace, not to the region enclosed by the
arc. These counts have disjoint supports. Put
                                                               bNin +Nout
                    M = Nn − Nin − Nout ≥ 0,           dQi =               dP.
                                                               EbNin +Nout
Every omitted straddle counted by M supplies a passage in one of a fixed number of shell
bands between ri−k and ri+k , allowing fixed buffer shifts. Indeed it is neither confined below
the lower cutoff nor confined above the upper one; its radial travel or its crossing between
opposite rays has substantial diameter near one of those levels. Distinct straddles give disjoint
subarcs. The small-box construction used for (7.3) therefore bounds M by a fixed sum of shell
passage counts.
    These counts have all fixed exponential moments under Qi . For a count near the inner
edge, condition on states outside an intermediate radius below the support of the outer tilt.
The outer factor is then constant, and the conditional law is the inner tilt with a compatible
exterior partition. Its edge moment is bounded by Proposition 7.4. For a count near the outer
edge, reverse this conditioning and use the inward version of that proposition. Middle buffers
miss both supports and use Proposition 7.3 directly. Hölder’s inequality over the fixed number
of counts gives
                                   EQi etM ≤ Ct     (t ≥ 0),                               (7.11)
uniformly in i, n and the exterior states. This argument uses conditional estimates, not
independence of the two tilts.
   Between the two tilt supports, choose two separated regular subbands. Proposition 6.15
constructs proper actual wall-to-wall paths of opposite colors in these bands. Their conditional
probabilities are bounded below uniformly after fixing states away from their supports; the
two tilt factors are measurable there. Applying the construction successively gives an event Ei
with
                                        Qi (Ei ) ≥ p > 0.
On Ei , at least one raw straddle lies between the two paths. Indeed raw strands cannot cross
either actual path. Along each flank, the end vertices of the two paths have opposite colors, so
the wall segment between them contains an odd number of raw wall ports. In the intervening
disk, pairings of ports on a single flank remove them in pairs. Hence at least one raw arc joins
the two flanks. It is trapped between the blockers and is a localized straddle.
    The full tilt ν by bNn is obtained from Qi by the density bM /EQi bM . Cauchy–Schwarz
yields
                               Qi (Ei )2 ≤ EQi [bM 1Ei ] EQi [b−M 1Ei ],

The last inequality follows from (7.11); because M ≥ 0, one of the two denominator factors is
at most one. Thus the same argument works for b < 1 and b > 1.

   The costs P, D∗ enter the charge comparison of Theorem 8.11. The finite matrix argument
next isolates the two decay powers. Section 8 proves their staggered physical instance and then
uses it in the scalar estimate of Theorem 8.8 and the geometric application in Corollary A.4.
The remaining arguments in this section concern finite matrices alone.

The scalar used to normalize a row is defined on the outer spectral strip, independently of any
Perron eigenvector. The use of an inversion relation together with symmetry and analyticity
follows the classical method developed by Baxter [3]. We define the scalar by its boundary
data and prove the identities needed here.

Lemma 7.9 (Outer-strip scalar). There is a unique nonvanishing holomorphic function F on
0 < ℜu < λ whose logarithmic modulus is the strip Poisson integral of
                                                                         !
                                                             sinh2 y
                           h(y) = 12 log r(iy) = 21 log   1+

on both boundary lines and for which F (λ/2) > 0. It extends locally across both boundary lines,

                F (ū) = F (u),    F (λ − u) = F (u),     F (u)F (−u) = r(u),            (7.16)



                                               109
and has F (0) = F (λ) = 1. The last identity continues F nonvanishingly to −λ < ℜu < 0, and
symmetry continues it to λ < ℜu < 2λ. On the common domain,

                                             F (u)F (λ + u) = r(u).                                 (7.17)

In the closed original strip, and in each closed substrip of −λ < ℜu < 2λ, log |F (u)| − |ℑu| is
bounded, with a bound depending on λ and the chosen substrip.
Proof. For 0 < x < λ, the two strip Poisson kernels are
                          sin(πx/λ)                                               sin(πx/λ)
    P0 (x, t) =                              ,              Pλ (x, t) =                              .
                  2λ{cosh(πt/λ) − cos(πx/λ)}                              2λ{cosh(πt/λ) + cos(πx/λ)}
Their masses are 1 − x/λ and x/λ, respectively. Their sum has a uniformly bounded first
absolute moment, and exponentially decaying tails. Thus
                                         Z
                          U (x + iy) =        {P0 (x, y − t) + Pλ (x, y − t)}h(t) dt
                                         R

is harmonic and U (x + iy) − |y| is bounded, since h(t) − |t| is bounded. A harmonic conjugate
on the simply connected strip defines F = exp(U + iV ). Fix its constant phase by F (λ/2) > 0.
This also proves uniqueness.
    The boundary data are real analytic. For example, near any point of the imaginary edge
choose the analytic branch of 12 log r(u) that is real on that edge. The difference of its real
part and U vanishes there, so odd harmonic reflection extends that difference across the edge.
This gives a local holomorphic extension of F , still nonvanishing. The other edge is identical.
Symmetries of the Poisson integral and the phase at λ/2 give the first two identities in (7.16).
On the imaginary edge, F (iy)F (−iy) = |F (iy)|2 = r(iy), so analytic continuation gives the
third identity.
    The only zeros of r closest to the imaginary axis are at u = ±λ; all its zeros are real.
Hence r(u)/F (−u) extends F without zeros to −λ < ℜu < 0. Reflection about λ/2 gives the
extension across the other edge and (7.17). Along the real interval [0, λ], F is real, nonzero,
and positive at the midpoint, hence positive throughout. At zero the third identity gives
F (0)2 = r(0) = 1; the endpoint values follow. The growth assertion on the extended substrips
follows from F (−u) = r(u)/F (u) and symmetry, because log |r(u)| − 2|ℑu| is bounded at the
two imaginary infinities on every bounded real interval. On a compact remaining rectangle
the extended function is continuous and nonzero.

Theorem 7.10 (Homogeneous angular sandwich). For every fixed λ in (7.13), let Bout,k and
Bin,k be finite products of Pξ,k and Pζ,k , respectively, at real arguments in [−v, v]. Suppose
that each product contains at least n ≥ 1 rows at argument zero. Then

                                     lim sup sup ∥Bout,k Nk (x)Bin,k ∥ ≤ Cn−1+ρ ,           (7.23)
                                      k→∞ x∈[−v,v]

               lim sup   sup     ∥Bout,k {Nk (x) − eiτ (x−y) Nk (y)}Bin,k ∥ ≤ Cn−1−ρ .      (7.24)
                k→∞ x,y∈[−v,v]

Immediately to either side of each Nk , one may insert an even number, bounded by a fixed L,
of additional rows with the corresponding twist, at arbitrary real arguments in [−v, v]. The two
terms in (7.24) may use different additional lists. The constant can then depend on L, as well
as on λ, but not on n or on the real arguments. Moreover,

                                   lim sup sup ∥Pζ,k (x)∥ ≤ 1.                              (7.25)
                                    k→∞ x∈[−v,v]

The limit superior is taken for each fixed finite setup: its row counts and any geometric
parameters are fixed before k → ∞.

    We call (7.23) the low-stencil (amplitude) bound and (7.24) the high-stencil (phase-
cancelled) bound. The latter removes the leading angular factor eiτ x ; these names concern
angular cancellation, not the spectral cutoff used below.
    We establish the theorem in finite-dimensional steps. The analytic argument only uses
scalar normal families obtained from these finite matrices; no common infinite-volume spectral
representation is needed.

Lemma 7.11 (Finite row identities). For every k and every unitary twist,

                         [Pζ,k (z), Pζ,k (w)] = 0,         Pζ,k (z)∗ = Pζ,k (−z̄).          (7.26)

In particular, this holomorphic family has a common orthonormal eigenbasis. For |ℜz| ≤ v,

                                         ∥AD

Proof. Let J = ( 01 10 ). Direct multiplication gives

                      R12 (x − y)R13 (x)R23 (y) = R23 (y)R13 (x)R12 (x − y),                (7.28)

where tp is transpose only in the auxiliary spin. For completeness, the nonconstant entries in
the one-minus-spin sector of (7.28) reduce to

                               b(x − y) + a(x)b(y) = b(x)a(y),

                               a(x) + b(x − y)b(y) = a(x − y)a(y).

These are sine addition identities. The all-equal-spin sectors are scalar, and spin reversal gives
the remaining sector. Equations (7.29) and (7.30) follow from their displayed 2-by-2 blocks.


                                                     111
    Slide the intertwiner R12 (x − y) across the two auxiliary rows using (7.28). Their boundary
tensor K(ζ) ⊗ K(ζ) commutes with the intertwiner because R preserves total spin. For
generic arguments the intertwiner is invertible, and cyclicity of the auxiliary trace proves row
commutativity. Analytic continuation removes the genericity restriction.
    On taking an adjoint, the order of the site factors reverses. Auxiliary partial transpose
reverses that order once more: its matrix entries belonging to distinct physical sites commute.
Crossing then gives, for any D,
                                    TkD (u)∗ = TkJ D̄J (λ − ū).
For a unitary diagonal twist JK(ζ)J = K(ζ). The symmetries of F prove the second identity
in (7.26). Commutativity with the adjoints makes each row normal and gives simultaneous
orthogonal diagonalization.                                                          p
    On u = iy, the real-coefficient formula for R and inversion show that R(iy)/ r(iy) is
unitary. Approduct of these matrices on the auxiliary and physical spins is unitary too. Since
|F (iy)| = r(iy) and auxiliary dimension is two, its partial trace with D has norm at most
2∥D∥. On u = λ + iy, crossing converts the traced row into a reversed product at −iy, with
the transpose and spin flip moved to D. The same bound follows. For each fixed k, the growth
of F and the explicit trigonometric entries of R show that the normalized matrix entries
are bounded throughout the strip. The bounded-strip maximum principle, applied to every
unit-vector matrix element, gives (7.27).

7.5   The fusion estimate
The essential gain is a bound on the three-dimensional auxiliary quotient in a two-row product.
It remains valid when d < 1. Fusion of shifted fundamental R-matrices at a degenerate
auxiliary channel is an established construction; Kulish, Reshetikhin, and Sklyanin develop it
in the rational GL(N ) setting [24, Section 2, equations (12)–(18)]. The trigonometric quotient
and its norm estimate below are proved directly.
Lemma 7.12 (A spin-one quotient). Use two auxiliary spins, labeled 1, 2, and one physical

has its only zero in the strip at zero, has modulus one on its boundary lines, and maps its

singularities there. Its entries are bounded at both imaginary infinities: bL/r is bounded there
and B tends to a number of modulus one. The strip maximum principle applied to its scalar
matrix elements gives (7.33).

Lemma 7.13 (Normalized two-row inversion). For u = ρ(v − z) with |ℜu| < λ/2, put
b(u) = | tan(πu/(2λ))|. Then
                                   ∥Pζ,k (z)Pζ,k (z − π) − I∥ ≤ Cb(u)k ,                  (7.35)
                   ∥Mk (z)Pζ,k (z − π) + Pξ,k (z)Mk (z − π)∥ ≤ C|eiτ ρz |b(u)k .          (7.36)
The continued rows are those defined by Lemma 7.9. The errors vanish uniformly on every
compact subset of this fusion strip. Equation (7.25) holds; more generally, the limiting norm
is at most one uniformly on compact subsets of the closed physical strip |ℜz| ≤ v.

                                                 113
Proof. The product of the two auxiliary row traces can be regrouped as the two-auxiliary
trace of j Gj (u). Indeed the factors R1i and R2j commute for i ̸= j, so no same-site order is
         Q

changed. The scalar denominator is r(u)k by (7.17).
    The twist K(ζ) ⊗ K(ζ) preserves the invariant line and acts on it as the identity. In a
basis beginning with that line, both this twist and every Gj are block upper triangular. The
trace therefore equals the invariant-line contribution I plus the trace of the quotient product
against a bounded auxiliary matrix. The off-diagonal block contributes exactly zero to the
trace, regardless of its size. Lemma 7.12 proves (7.35).
    For the marker use
                                 Q = D ⊗ K(ζ) + e−iτ λ K(ξ) ⊗ D.
If D = |−⟩⟨+| and τ = ϵ, then

                               Qs0 = (ζ −1 + e−iϵλ ξ −1 )| − −⟩ = 0.

If D = |+⟩⟨−| and τ = −ϵ, then

                                  Qs0 = (ζ + eiϵλ ξ)| + +⟩ = 0.

Both cancellations use (7.20). Thus Q kills the invariant line. Its product with the block
triangular fusion product again has zero contribution from the off-diagonal block, and now its
invariant-line contribution is zero as well. Taking its quotient trace and extracting eiτ ρz proves
(7.36).
    On the right edge z = v + iy, one has z − π = −z̄. Consequently (7.26) turns (7.35) into
approximate unitarity. On the left edge apply the same identity at z + π. The errors converge
to zero uniformly for y in a fixed compact interval. To pass into the strip, truncate at |ℑz| = Y .
On the two vertical sides the norm is at most 1 + ok (1); on the horizontal sides the uniform
bound is two. The harmonic measure of the horizontal sides tends uniformly to zero on any
fixed compact subset as Y → ∞. The maximum principle for unit-vector matrix elements, first
with k → ∞ and then Y → ∞, proves the claimed limiting contraction. The edge estimates
include the real endpoints.

7.6    Scalar limits and continuation through the strip edges
Throughout this subsection, 0 < ρ < 1/2 is fixed. Every limit in the number k of sites is taken
with the finite list of rows, its real arguments, and all spectral cutoffs fixed. In particular,
estimates in the damping parameter n are obtained only after this limit. Constants may
depend on ρ and on the permitted bound for the number of additional rows, but not on k or n.
    Put S = {z : |ℜz| < v}, where v = π/2, and use w = eiz to identify S with the right
half-plane. The branch of z = −i log w in this identification is always the one with |ℜz| < v.
For a fixed twist, all the rows have a common orthonormal eigenbasis. Their eigenvalues are
therefore holomorphic functions of z; the same basis diagonalizes their analytic continuations,
by the identity theorem. The adjoint identity (7.26) for the rows becomes

                                  fk (w) = fk (w)     (ℜw > 0).                             (7.37)

In particular, fk (1) is real. Here and below P (0) refers to the argument z = 0, which
corresponds to w = 1.
Lemma 7.14 (Scalar edge continuation). Fix s > 0. From any sequence of row eigenvalues
satisfying |fk (1)| ≥ e−s one can extract a subsequence converging locally uniformly in the right
half-plane to a holomorphic function f that is not identically zero. It satisfies

                            |f | ≤ 1,    f (1) ∈ R,      |f (1)| ≥ e−s .

                                               114
Its only possible zeros in the right half-plane lie on the positive real axis. At every nonzero
point of the imaginary axis, f extends holomorphically and without zeros through the axis, and
has modulus one there. Along the chosen subsequence, the eigenvalues and their inverses are
locally bounded through each such boundary point.
Proof. The row bound (7.27) gives a normal family in S. The lower bound at 1 prevents its
limit from being identically zero. The asymptotic contraction (7.25) gives |f | ≤ 1 throughout
the strip: its boundary version, together with the uniform row bound, can first be applied on
a finite rectangle, and the harmonic measure of the two horizontal sides tends to zero as the
rectangle grows. Equivalently, one can use the interior contraction already obtained from this
argument. Equation (7.37) passes to the limit.
    The inversion estimate (7.35) makes fk zero-free on every compact subset of each spectral
band about a strip edge, for all sufficiently large k. Under u = ρ(v − z), the band |ℜu| < λ/2
about the right edge is 0 < ℜz < π; the corresponding band about the left edge is −π < ℜz < 0.
These two bands cover the strict strip except for its center line. Hurwitz’s theorem therefore
excludes zeros of f off that center line, which maps to the positive real axis in the w-plane.
    We give the additional argument needed at an edge; mere convergence on the strict strip
would not suffice. Fix a disk D centered at z0 = v + iy0 , with closure compactly contained in
the right-edge spectral band. In this paragraph write fk (z) for fk (eiz ). On D,

                                 qk (z) := fk (z)fk (z − π) −→ 1

uniformly. For all sufficiently large k, choose the analytic square root of qk near 1, and set
                                   fk (z)
                          gk (z) = p       ,     Uk (z) = − log |gk (z)|.
                                    qk (z)
Both eigenvalue factors are nonzero on D. On its vertical diameter the adjoint identity gives
qk (z) = |fk (z)|2 , so that |gk (z)| = 1. Consequently Uk is harmonic on D and odd under
reflection in that diameter. On the physical half of D, the row bound and qk → 1 give
Uk ≥ −C. At a fixed point strictly inside that half-disk, Uk is bounded above as well: the
limiting eigenvalue has no zero there by the preceding paragraph.
    For clarity, the elementary boundary estimate used here is as follows. For a harmonic
function U odd across a diameter of a disk, its Poisson formula is an integral over one semicircle
with kernel
                                    K(z, t) = P (z, t) − P (z, tref ),
where P is the disk Poisson kernel and tref is the reflected boundary point. This kernel is
nonnegative when z and t are on the same side of the diameter. On any strictly smaller
half-disk,
                                 0 ≤ K(z, t) ≤ CK(z∗ , t)                            (7.38)
for a fixed interior radial point z∗ on that side. The assertion follows directly from the Poisson
formula: the difference has the product of the two signed distances to the diameter in its
numerator, and its remaining denominators are bounded above and below on the smaller disk.
If U ≥ −C on the physical half-disk, the negative part of its semicircle data has a bounded
contribution. The formula at z∗ therefore bounds the positive contribution there, and (7.38)
bounds it at all points of the smaller half-disk. Reflection supplies the other half.
    Applying this observation on an intermediate disk inside D bounds |Uk | on a smaller closed
disk. Thus fk and fk−1 are uniformly bounded there. Normal families extend the already
chosen interior limit across the edge, and the limit has modulus one on the diameter. This
extension is unique, so taking further subsequences does not change it. The left edge is treated
with the shifted inversion identity. These two arguments cover every nonzero point of the
imaginary axis.

                                               115
7.7   Almost constant low spectral modes
The outer row products in Theorem 7.10 damp eigenvalues whose modulus at zero stays below
one. We therefore first analyze the complementary spectral subspace, on which that modulus
is at least e−s . The next lemma shows that its row eigenvalues remain close to one of the two
real signs over the full physical angle interval.

Lemma 7.15 (Half-annulus and sign estimates). There are constants c0 , s0 , C > 0 such that
the following holds for 0 < s ≤ s0 . Define

                  As = {w : c−1
                             0 s < |w| < c0 /s},            A+
                                                             s = As ∩ {ℜw ≥ 0}.

Every scalar limit in Lemma 7.14 obeys

       C −1 ≤ |f (w)| ≤ C    (w ∈ A+
                                   s ),         f (w) = σ + O(s)       (|w| = 1, ℜw ≥ 0),   (7.39)

where σ = sgn f (1) and the boundary values use its continuation. The same assertions hold,
in operator norm and with a limsup as k → ∞, for the diagonal compressions

                    Eζ,k (s) = 1{|Pζ (0)|≥e−s } ,     pζ,k (w) = Pζ (z)|ran Eζ,k (s) .

For the sign assertion, restrict further to either sign of Pζ (0). The conclusions also hold for
any further spectral restriction. The compressed rows and their inverses are locally bounded in
neighborhoods of the two boundary rays of As . The same statements apply to the twist ξ.

Proof. Multiply f by its sign at 1 and put a = |f (1)|. For a nonconstant function, Schwarz–Pick
in the right half-plane gives, for r > 0,

                                     σf (r) − a         |r − 1|
                                                 ≤ t :=         .
                                     1 − aσf (r)         r+1

Writing the fraction on the left as h, one obtains
                                           1−h             1+t
                |1 − σf (r)| = (1 − a)           ≤ (1 − a)     ≤ s max(r, r−1 ).
                                          1 + ah           1−t
Constant functions satisfy the same conclusion directly. Choose c0 sufficiently small. This
estimate excludes zeros on the positive real axis even on an annulus with inner radius c−1
                                                                                        0 s/4
and outer radius 4c0 /s. Lemma 7.14 excludes zeros elsewhere in its right half. On that
half-annulus, therefore,
                                    U (w) := − log |f (w)|
is a nonnegative harmonic function, vanishing on the imaginary-axis boundary segments and
extending oddly through them. On the positive real axis the same estimate gives

                                       U (r) ≤ Cs max(r, r−1 ).                             (7.40)

    Fix r ∈ [c−1
              0 s, c0 /s] and rescale by r. A bounded Harnack chain inside {1/4 < |w| <
4, ℜw > 0} controls U on the unit semicircle away from its endpoints by CU (r). Near either
endpoint, use a disk centered on the imaginary axis, of fixed radius after rescaling. Its interior
reference point is controlled by the same Harnack chain; the reflected Poisson estimate (7.38),
now with nonnegative data on the physical half, controls the smaller half-disk up to the
diameter. Consequently

                       0 ≤ U (w) ≤ Cs max(r, r−1 )        (|w| = r, ℜw ≥ 0).                (7.41)


                                                    116
The right side is bounded by a fixed constant on the asserted annulus, proving its uniform
inverse bound.
    For r in a fixed neighborhood of 1, the bound is Cs. After odd reflection it bounds |U | in
fixed neighborhoods of the two unit semicircle endpoints as well. Harmonic gradient estimates
therefore give |∇U | ≤ Cs in a neighborhood of the whole unit semicircle. Choose a branch
of log(σf ) in a thin simply connected neighborhood of that semicircle, with value log a at 1.
The Cauchy–Riemann equations identify the modulus of its derivative with |∇U |. Integration
along the semicircle gives log(σf ) = O(s), and hence the second assertion in (7.39).
    We spell out why these scalar arguments are uniform for the growing matrices. If any
compressed diagonal norm assertion failed, choose an offending eigenvalue, a point in the
indicated compact set, and a subsequence of site numbers. The points have a convergent
subsequence. Lemma 7.14 supplies a scalar limit, including local convergence through an edge
when necessary; (7.39) then contradicts the failure. For the sign assertion one first fixes the
sign subsequence. The same argument rules out zeros of the finite compressed eigenvalues on
the compact half-annulus for all sufficiently large k, so that their inverses are defined there.
No bound on the number of eigenvalues is used.
    Finally, scalar continuation across an imaginary boundary interval with modulus one is
given by Schwarz reflection, f (w) = 1/f (−w) on its other side. The just-proved inverse bounds
therefore control this continuation near either ray, as well as its inverse. The edge-continuation
argument and the same subsequence contradiction give the corresponding local bounds for the
finite compressed matrices. One may decrease c0 once to keep all neighborhoods inside the
wider annulus used above.

7.8   Marker continuation and its two leading modes
The diagonal row estimates do not directly bound a marker between the two twist spaces.
The fusion identity continues its scalar matrix elements around an annulus. Their Laurent
coefficients then separate the leading angular mode from the remainder canceled by a high
stencil.
Lemma 7.16 (Annular marker continuation). Let Eξ , Eζ be the low spectral projections from
Lemma 7.15, possibly further spectrally restricted, and put

                              mk (w) = Eξ M (z)Eζ ,       w = eiz .

For any sequence of unit vectors ak ∈ ran Eξ and bk ∈ ran Eζ , one can extract a subsequence
for which ⟨ak , mk (w)bk ⟩ converges on the right half of As to the restriction of a holomorphic
function m on the full open annulus As . Convergence includes the closed right half of every
strictly smaller annulus, using continuation at its imaginary boundary points. The limit
satisfies
                                 |m(w)| ≤ C|w|τ ρ      (w ∈ As ).                          (7.42)
On the left half-annulus its values are the limits of the same matrix elements of
                                       −1             −1
                    mext
                     k (−w) = −pξ,k (w) mk (w)pζ,k (w) ,              ℜw > 0.              (7.43)

Proof. The row bound (7.27), including the scalar factor in M , gives

                                       ∥mk (w)∥ ≤ C|w|τ ρ

on the physical right half-plane. Lemma 7.15 gives the same limsup bound, up to a constant,
for (7.43). Thus the scalar matrix elements of both expressions are normal families on their
respective half-annuli. It remains to check that their limits glue through both imaginary rays,
including local boundedness there.

                                               117
    Use z coordinates near z = v + iy. Compressing the fusion identity (7.36) gives

                          mk (z)pζ,k (z − π) + pξ,k (z)mk (z − π) = o(1)                   (7.44)

uniformly on compact neighborhoods, in operator norm. Here mk (z) abbreviates the continued
compression at argument z, and the same convention applies to p. The error tends to zero for
each fixed s and compact neighborhood. The inversion identity also gives

                                 pξ,k (z) = pξ,k (z − π)−1 + o(1),

and the analogous relation for the other twist. All these inverses are locally bounded by
Lemma 7.15. In a small neighborhood of the edge, one of z, z − π is in the physical closed
strip. Solving (7.44) for the other marker shows that both continued marker expressions are
locally bounded. Solving for mk (z) in particular gives

                    mk (z) = −pξ,k (z − π)−1 mk (z − π)pζ,k (z − π)−1 + o(1).

Since ei(z−π) = −eiz , this is exactly the compatibility of the physical and external expressions
across the upper imaginary ray. Across the lower ray use the same identity with z + π in
place of z and solve in the opposite direction. Again one argument is physical, and inversion
produces precisely (7.43).
    Montel’s theorem and a diagonal subsequence over compact sets now give the asserted
holomorphic function on the full annulus. This uses scalar matrix elements only: the two twist
spaces may have different eigenbases and dimensions tending to infinity. There is no assertion
that limits of individual matrix factors can be multiplied. Their finite-dimensional identities
and uniform norm bounds are used before taking subsequences. Bound (7.42) follows on each
half-annulus from its defining expression, and on the rays by continuation.

Lemma 7.17 (Low-band estimates). For 0 < s ≤ s0 , let Eξ , Eζ be as in Lemma 7.16.
Uniformly for real x, y ∈ [−v, v],

                                           lim sup ∥Eξ N (x)Eζ ∥ ≤ Cs1−ρ ,                 (7.45)
                                            k→∞
                                              iτ (x−y)
                       lim sup ∥Eξ {N (x) − e            N (y)}Eζ ∥ ≤ Cs1+ρ .              (7.46)
                         k→∞

The estimates persist if each occurrence of N is multiplied, immediately on each side separately,
by a product of an even bounded number of the corresponding twist rows at real arguments in
[−v, v]. Each of the four lists has even length; the lists for the two occurrences may differ.

Proof. Expand the scalar limit in Lemma 7.16 as m(w) = j∈Z bj wj . Cauchy’s estimate on
                                                                   P

circles just inside the two annular radii, with a fixed proportional margin, gives

                                       |bj | ≤ C(C ′ s)|j−τ ρ| .                           (7.47)

For example, use the outer circle when j > τ ρ and the inner circle when j < τ ρ. Shrinking s0
makes C ′ s0 < 1/2. Because 0 < ρ < 1/2, summing these bounds on the unit circle yields

                       |m(w)| ≤ Csρ ,                                                      (7.48)
               m(w) − m(−w)
                              = bτ wτ + O(s1+ρ ),                      |bτ | ≤ Cs1−ρ .     (7.49)
                     2
Indeed j = 0 has exponent ρ, the nearest nonzero exponent is 1 − ρ, and among odd indices
other than τ the smallest exponent is 1 + ρ. The remaining geometric tails are uniform.


                                                 118
   By the arbitrary-unit-vector subsequence argument in Lemma 7.16, (7.48) gives

                                lim sup     sup         ∥mk (w)∥ ≤ Csρ .                       (7.50)
                                 k→∞ |w|=1, ℜw≥0

The supremum causes no difficulty: choose offending points, pass to a convergent subsequence
on the compact semicircle, and use the local uniform convergence, including at its endpoints.
   Further split each low spectral projection according to the sign of P (0), and first work on
one pair of sign blocks, with signs σξ , σζ ∈ {1, −1}. Lemma 7.15 gives, in limsup uniformly on
the physical unit semicircle,

                          pξ,k = σξ I + O(s),            pζ,k = σζ I + O(s),

and the same estimates for their inverses. Combining (7.43) and (7.50), every scalar subse-
quential limit satisfies
                            m(−w) = −σξ σζ m(w) + O(s1+ρ ).                          (7.51)
The compressed anticommutator is exactly

                          Eξ N (z)Eζ = mk (z)pζ,k (z) + pξ,k (z)mk (z).

If the signs disagree, its leading terms cancel and it is O(s1+ρ ) in limsup. If both signs equal
σ, (7.51) and (7.49) instead give, in each tested scalar subsequential limit,

                                 Eξ N (x)Eζ = 2σbτ eiτ x + O(s1+ρ ).                           (7.52)

This equation denotes the corresponding scalar matrix element, not an identification of spaces
of different dimensions. Its leading term has size O(s1−ρ ) and cancels between x and y after
multiplication by eiτ (x−y) . Applying the same argument to arbitrary sequences of test vectors
and of x, y proves (7.45)–(7.46). Summing the four sign blocks changes the constant only.
    Finally, an even product of at most a fixed number of twist rows equals I + O(s) on its sign
block, because every factor equals σI + O(s). Multiplying the compressed anticommutator by
such products changes it by at most Cs∥mk ∥ in limsup, hence by Cs1+ρ . This reasoning is
separate for the two terms, so their additional lists need not agree. All these additional rows
commute with the spectral projections on their own side. This proves the last assertion.

7.9   Damping the remaining spectral bands
Proof of Theorem 7.10. The row contraction is (7.25). It remains to insert the two outer
products in the low-band estimates. For either twist write

                                          hk = − log |P (0)|,

with value +∞ on the kernel. The spectral projections of hk commute with every row of their
twist. If a band has positive lower endpoint t, the at least n zero-argument factors in its outer
product give the bound e−nt on that band. The other factors have asymptotic norm at most
one. Since their list is finite and fixed while k → ∞, the full product has limsup norm at most
e−nt there. On a band without a positive lower endpoint its limsup norm is at most one by
contraction of all the factors.
    For n large enough, choose J so that 2J /n ≤ s0 < 2J+1 /n, and split each side into

        I0 = (−∞, 1/n],         Ii = (2i−1 /n, 2i /n]     (1 ≤ i ≤ J),     I∞ = (2J /n, +∞].
                          i−1
Set D0 = 1 and Di = e−2         for i ≥ 1. For a pair of finite bands Ii , Ij , apply Lemma 7.17 with

                                               max(2i , 2j )
                                          s=                 .
                                                   n

                                                  119
Both band projections are further restrictions of the appropriate low projections. Their outer
products contribute Di Dj . For either exponent a ∈ {1 − ρ, 1 + ρ}, the triangle inequality
therefore bounds the sum of these blocks by
                                       J
                               Cn−a           Di Dj max(2i , 2j )a ≤ Ca n−a .
                                       X
                                                                                                    (7.53)
                                      i,j=0

The infinite double sum converges, since the Di decrease exponentially in 2i . In particular
there is no factor counting the number of bands.
     The remainder has hk > 2J /n > s0 /2 and therefore contributes e−ns0 /2 on its side. Group
all blocks with a remainder on the left into a single compression, and then all remaining blocks
with a remainder on the right into another. The unrestricted row bounds control N and its
two-term difference; contraction controls the outer product on the other side. Thus these two
groups together contribute at most Ce−ns0 /2 in limsup. Additional bounded row lists cause
only a fixed constant, and their low-band contribution was already covered by Lemma 7.17.
     Taking a = 1−ρ in (7.53) proves the first asserted sandwich bound. Taking a = 1+ρ proves
the second, with its phase eiτ (x−y) and with independently chosen additional even row lists.
The bounded range of smaller n follows directly from contraction of the outer products and the
uniform marker bound, after enlarging the constant. At every stage the band decomposition
is finite before taking the site-number limsup; only the resulting scalar estimates are then
summed and bounded uniformly in n.


8      Physical rows and signed charge comparison
The finite-circle estimate of Section 7 is normalized by a holomorphic scalar defined on a strip.
We first show that this scalar agrees asymptotically with the Perron normalization of physical
square-lattice rows. The proof combines a staggered finite matrix calculation with the periodic
six-vertex pressure. The disk-derived screen estimates then identify square-plane limits of the
reflected diagrams and turn the matrix bounds into current estimates. Finally, the passage
and arm estimates control the signed boundary weights in the charge comparison needed for
the observable. Throughout this section,
                           π    π                                               λ
                             <λ< ,             d = 2 cos λ ∈ (0, 1),      ρ=      .
                           3    2                                               π
Circumference limits are always taken with the lattice and every insertion diagram fixed. Mesh
limits are taken only afterwards.

8.1     Staggered rows and their positive metric
We use the functions R, a, b, c, r, F of Section 7, with π/3 < λ < π/2, d = 2 cos λ, ρ = λ/π,
and v = π/2. The finite algebra and estimates below also hold throughout 0 < λ < π/2. In
particular,
               F (u) = F (u),        F (λ − u) = F (u),    F (u)F (−u) = r(u).           (8.1)
The last identity continues F to −λ < ℜu < 0; reflection about λ/2 continues it past the other
edge. These continuations are nonzero in the regions used below.
   Put δ∗ = λ/4. For even N , let HN = (C2 )⊗N and set

Lemma 8.1 (Exchange, adjoint, and strip bound). For x − y = λ/2 one has
                                                −1

                                      stag     ∗G       stag

                          ∥AD
                            N,stag (z)∥GN ≤ 2∥D∥,               −v ≤ ℜz ≤ v.                   (8.7)
The constant is independent of even N .
Proof. Multiply the Yang–Baxter identity on the two site spaces by their exchange P12 . Since
this exchange interchanges the site labels, the result is (8.5); invertibility holds at x − y = λ/2
by positivity of C.
    We first record the adjoint calculation for arbitrary D. Let J = ( 01 10 ) reverse the two basis
states of the single auxiliary spin, and put D♯ = JDJ. Taking the ordinary adjoint reverses
the site order. Auxiliary transpose reverses it back, because the entries belonging to distinct
site spaces commute. Applying Jp R(u)tp Jp = R(λ − u) gives a forward row with arguments
λ − uj (U ) and boundary matrix D♯ . These arguments are the exchanged stagger at central
argument λ − U . Applying (8.5) on each complete pair gives
                                                            ♯
                                 G−1 D     ∗      D
                                  N TN (U ) GN = TN (λ − U ).                                  (8.8)
Equation (8.1) gives the same transformation for Φ. For a unitary diagonal twist K(ζ)♯ = K(ζ),
proving (8.6). The usual two-auxiliary Yang–Baxter calculation applies with these fixed site
offsets: the difference of the two row arguments is the same at every site, and K(ζ) ⊗ K(ζ)
commutes with the auxiliary intertwiner. Taking the auxiliary traces proves commutativity.
The adjoint belongs to the same commuting family, so the family consists of commuting normal
matrices in the GN inner product.
    For the bound, take ℜU = 0 and write x = U + δ∗ , y = U − δ∗ . Then x = −y and
y = −x. For E = Rp1 (x)Rp2 (y), taking the transpose of the exchange identity and using
R(t)R(−t) = r(t) id gives
                               E ∗C E = r(x)r(y) id = |Φ(U )|2 id .                            (8.9)
The last equality follows by multiplying the four factors in |F (x)F (y)|2 and using (8.1). The
normalized pair is consequently unitary for the metric idp ⊗C. Embedded in the full space, it
is unitary for idp ⊗GN , since all other factors of GN act on disjoint sites. The product over
pairs has norm one. Auxiliary trace with D costs at most 2∥D∥, proving the bound on ℜU = 0.
Equation (8.8) proves it on ℜU = λ, because ∥D♯ ∥ = ∥D∥.
    For fixed N , each normalized matrix entry is bounded as |ℑU | → ∞: each R factor grows
at most as exp(|ℑU |), while log |F (U ± δ∗ )| = |ℑU | + O(1), also on the continued portions
by (8.1). The strip maximum principle applied to matrix elements between GN -unit vectors
proves (8.7) throughout the strip.

Lemma 8.2 (Staggered fusion bound). Put ϕ(U ) = tan(πU/(2λ)), α = tan(π/8), and

The norm includes the fixed Euclidean norm on the quotient auxiliary space. In particular the

Proof. Applying (8.5) to each auxiliary row separately, and commuting factors acting on
disjoint tensor factors before and after each application, exchanges the arguments in a pair of
fused site matrices. The invariant line is in the auxiliary spaces, so this identity passes to the
quotient. The scalar product r(x)r(y) is unchanged by exchange. Hence, for x − y = λ/2,
                                                  −1
                                 C12 Y1 (x)Y2 (y)C12 = Y1 (y)Y2 (x).                        (8.14)

    We give the reflected inversion identities needed at the two edges. Let Z(u) = Y (u)taux ,
where the transpose is in the rescaled three-dimensional auxiliary basis. Direct multiplication
of (8.11) gives the meromorphic identities

                       Y (u)T Y (−λ − u) = id,             Z(u)T Z(λ − u) = id .            (8.15)

Here T transposes both auxiliary and site indices. For completeness, if w = −λ − u, then
b(w) = −c(u), c(w) = −b(u), and a(u)a(w) = d − b(u)c(u). A mixed block of L(u)T L(w)
therefore equals (d − bc) id; the product of the scalar factors b/(ac) is 1/(a(u)a(w)). The
unmixed entries of Y (u) and Y (w) are −b/c and −c/b. This proves the first identity. For
w = λ − u, one has a(w) = b(u), b(w) = a(u), and c(u)c(w) = d + a(u)b(u). After a fixed
                                   taux have diagonal entries (−a, −b) and opposite off-diagonal

entries ± d. Their transpose products equal (d + ab) id, canceled by the scalar product
1/(c(u)c(w)); the unmixed entries are b/a and a/b. This proves the second identity.

                 Q(U )∗ = Qs (U )−1 = CQ(U )−1 C −1 ,              Q(U )∗C Q(U ) = id .

If ℜU = λ/2, put H(U ) = Q(U )taux = Z2 (U − δ∗ )Z1 (U + δ∗ ). The order reversal is legitimate

    The complete quotient product is therefore unitary in the metric idC3 ⊗GN on the left edge.
On the right edge its auxiliary transpose, a reversed product of these unitary pairs, is unitary.
Undoing a transpose in a three-dimensional tensor factor costs at most three in operator norm.
                                                                                             1/2
Indeed, its block-matrix expansion and Cauchy–Schwarz give this bound; conjugation by GN
commutes with auxiliary transpose, so the same bound holds for the present norm. The strip
maximum principle yields a uniform bound in between.
    There are no poles from r(uj (U )) on this closed strip, since |ℜuj (U )| ≤ 3λ/4 < λ. Also
Y (u) has a scalar factor b(u) and hence vanishes at u = 0. The complete product consequently
has a zero of order at least N/2 at each of U = δ∗ and U = −δ∗ . The two functions
in (8.12) have precisely these zeros, modulus one on the strip edges, and modulus less than
one inside. Dividing the product by B+ (U )N/2 B− (U )N/2 removes these zeros. For fixed N the
quotient remains bounded at the two imaginary ends of the strip, as follows directly from the
trigonometric formulas. Applying the maximum principle again proves (8.13).

8.3     Damping at the physical angles
Proposition 8.3 (Staggered angular estimate). Let γ0 = π/4. In the notation (8.16)–(8.17),
let Bout and Bin be fixed finite products of ξ-twist and ζ-twist rows, respectively, at real

                                                  123
arguments in [−v, v]. Suppose that each product includes at least n ≥ 1 rows at arguments in
{−γ0 , γ0 }. Uniformly for x, y ∈ [−v, v],

                                      lim sup ∥Bout NNstag (x)Bin ∥GN ≤ Cλ n−1+ρ ,         (8.21)
                                       N →∞
                                       N even
                lim sup ∥Bout NNstag (x) − eiτ (x−y)
                                                       NNstag (y) Bin ∥GN ≤ Cλ n−1−ρ .
                                                               
                                                                                           (8.22)
                N →∞
                N even

Immediately on either side of either marker sum one may insert a product of an even number
of the corresponding twist rows, at arbitrary real arguments in [−v, v]. The products may differ
between the two terms; the constant may depend on a common bound for these additional
counts. Every finite setup is fixed before the limit in N is taken.

Proof. The three changes from the homogeneous argument are the GN metric, the damping
basepoint eiγ0 , and the two-zero fusion error in (8.13). We give the analytic argument to track
these changes explicitly. All diagonalizations and projections in this proof are orthogonal for
GN ; no comparison with the original Euclidean norm is used. Suppress the superscript stag.
    Map the open z strip to the right half-plane by w = eiz , using the branch with −v < ℜz < v.
In a common eigenbasis of a fixed twist, let fN (w) be any eigenvalue. It is holomorphic and
uniformly bounded. The adjoint identity gives fN (w) = fN (w) and hence fN (1) ∈ R. For
a fixed small s > 0 restrict to eigenvalues satisfying |fN (eiγ0 )| ≥ e−s . Every sequence has a
subsequence converging on compact subsets to a nonzero holomorphic function f with |f | ≤ 1.
Nonzeroness follows at eiγ0 , and the bound follows from the edge unitarity and strip bound
proved above. Schwarz–Pick between eiγ0 and 1 gives, with α = tan(γ0 /2) < 1,

                                             e−s − α
                                 |f (1)| ≥            ≥ 1 − Cλ s.                          (8.23)
                                             1 − αe−s
The first inequality is the elementary extremal modulus bound for a disk of pseudohyperbolic
radius α centered at f (eiγ0 ); take s small enough that e−s > α. Thus a large eigenvalue at the
physical angle is also close in modulus to a real sign at the center.
   The remaining continuation argument uses the same analytic facts as Lemmas 7.14–7.17.
We verify the correspondence before applying their proofs:
   (i) The fixed-twist rows are commuting normal holomorphic matrices in the GN inner
       product, with adjoint reflection (8.6) and the uniform strip bound (8.7).
  (ii) The two-row and marker inversion errors in (8.18)–(8.19) tend uniformly to zero on
       compact subsets of the same edge bands 0 < ℜz < π and −π < ℜz < 0 used in the
       homogeneous proof. The two zeros in the fused error change its rate of convergence, not
       these bands or the resulting identities.
 (iii) The marker has the same growth factor |w|τ ρ , and (8.23) bounds the limiting row modulus
       at w = 1 below by 1 − Cλ s.
These are precisely the inputs to the scalar continuation and Laurent estimates. All their finite
matrix elements can be taken between GN -unit vectors: the proofs use only the indicated
intrinsic norms, not a bounded comparison between GN and the Euclidean metric.
    Here are their consequences with the present spectral cutoff. Define

                          Eη,N (s) = 1{|PN,η (γ0 )|≥e−s } ,     η ∈ {ξ, ζ}.

By the adjoint identity, the moduli at γ0 and −γ0 agree on every common eigenvector. The
scalar limits on this low subspace are zero-free off the positive real w axis by inversion and
Hurwitz’s theorem. Dividing by the analytic square root of the two-row product gives modulus
one on each strip edge; the reflected half-disk estimate from Lemma 7.14 bounds the continued

                                                 124
eigenvalues and their inverses there. Thus the scalar limits extend across the two imaginary
rays.
    For σ = sgn f (1), Schwarz–Pick based at 1 gives

                             |f (r) − σ| ≤ Cλ s max(r, r−1 ),       r > 0.                      (8.24)

Consequently the half-annulus proof, with Cλ s in place of s, gives bounded inverses on

                                 c−1
                                  0 s < |w| < c0 /s,         ℜw ≥ 0,                            (8.25)

and the sign estimate

                            f (w) = σ + Oλ (s),         |w| = 1,   ℜw ≥ 0.                      (8.26)

The constants absorb this fixed rescaling of s. An offending-eigenvalue subsequence would
contradict these scalar estimates, so they hold in limsup for the diagonal compressions
pη,N = PN,η |ran Eη,N (s) as well. For sufficiently small fixed s and all large N , their center values
are nonzero by (8.23); hence we may refine each projection according to the sign of PN,η (0).
Further spectral restrictions preserve every estimate.
    Compress the marker to mN = Eξ,N (s)MN Eζ,N (s). On the left half of the annulus its
continuation is represented, at −w with ℜw > 0, by

                                  −pξ,N (w)−1 mN (w)pζ,N (w)−1 .                                (8.27)

The marker inversion identity glues this expression to the physical one across both rays, exactly
as in Lemma 7.16. The inverse bounds control its errors. For every sequence of pairs of
GN -unit test vectors, Montel’s theorem therefore gives a scalar subsequential limit on the full
annulus with |m(w)| ≤ Cλ |w|τ ρ . No products of limiting operators on varying spaces are used.
    Writing m(w) = j∈Z βj wj , Cauchy’s estimates give |βj | ≤ Cλ (Cλ s)|j−τ ρ| and hence
                     P

There are only four sign blocks. On each one, an additional bounded even row product is

estimates with the allowed additional lists, even when the two terms use different lists.
     Finally decompose each side spectrally according to hη,N = − log |PN,η (γ0 )|, assigning value
+∞ at a zero. Use the band (−∞, n−1 ], successive dyadic bands up to a fixed sufficiently small
s0 , and the remainder. On a band with positive lower endpoint t, each damping row at either
physical angle has norm at most e−t , since the two moduli agree. Commutativity and (8.20)
show that the whole exterior product has limsup norm at most e−nt on that band; on the first
band use 1. For any pair of small bands use (8.29) or (8.30) with s the larger upper endpoint.
The double dyadic sum is bounded by Cλ n−β for β = 1 − ρ or 1 + ρ, respectively: after writing
                                                                                        j  k
each upper endpoint as 2j /n, its summands are bounded by Cλ n−β 2β max(j,k) e−c(2 +2 ) , with
the first band interpreted as having no exponential cost. Terms involving a remainder are
exponentially small in n, by the uniform strip bound and damping at least e−ns0 . This proves
both assertions.

8.4    The two physical steps
At z = γ0 the staggered arguments are λ/2, 0; at z = −γ0 they are λ, λ/2. Write R∗ = R(λ/2),
and let the cyclic shift on the spin basis be

                                 S|s1 , s2 , . . . , sN ⟩ = |s2 , . . . , sN , s1 ⟩.

For clarity define the unnormalized, untwisted physical rows by

                           TN (+) = TNid (λ/4),              TN (−) = TNid (3λ/4).

Lemma 8.4 (Physical rows and Perron vectors). The physical rows satisfy

                               TN (+) = SGN ,               TN (−) = S −1 GN .                      (8.31)

For an arbitrary auxiliary matrix D, the marked rows are physical rows with a single seam-edge
insertion:
                         TND (λ/4) = DN TN (+),
                                                                                       !
                                                                                   0 1              (8.32)
                         TND (3λ/4) = (JDT J)1 TN (−),                    J=           .
                                                                                   1 0
The subscript specifies the physical spin factor on which the matrix acts, and T denotes ordinary
transpose. With the periodic seam anchored in a fixed physical chart, sites N and 1 are its two
neighboring sites; the insertion support stays fixed as N grows. The untwisted rows TN (+) and
TN (−) are mutually adjoint for GN , commute, and are normal. Both preserve each total-spin
sector and are primitive on that sector. Let ΛN = ∥TN (+)∥GN = ∥TN (−)∥GN . In every sector
whose Perron eigenvalue equals ΛN , there is a unique positive GN -unit vector Ω; it satisfies

                    S 2 Ω = Ω,        TN (±)Ω = ΛN Ω,                  Ω∗ GN S ±1 = ΛN Ω∗ .         (8.33)

All other eigenvalues in such a sector have strictly smaller modulus. The scalar normalization
in (8.3) at either physical angle is F (λ/2)N/2 .

Proof. We verify the collapse directly in components. Spin labels take values ±1. The collapsed
vertices have entries

                       R(0)p,d;q,c = 1p=c 1d=q ,           R(λ)p,d;q,c = 1p=−d 1q=−c .

At the square vertex the equality a(λ/2) = b(λ/2) gives

                                    (R∗ )−c0 ,d0 ;−d1 ,c1 = Cd0 ,d1 ;c0 ,c1 .                       (8.34)

Each identity follows by listing the six nonzero entries of R. In the auxiliary trace use indices
p0 , p1 , . . . , pN = p0 so that the site j factor is (R(uj ))pj−1 ,dj ;pj ,cj . For the positive step the
even sites force p2j−1 = c2j and p2j = d2j , giving, with cyclic indices,

step the odd sites force p2j−2 = −d2j−1 and p2j−1 = −c2j−1 ; hence

                                        N/2

                                                       126
by (8.34). All auxiliary labels are fixed in these sums, so neither collapse introduces an
additional cycle factor. This proves (8.31).
    For a general auxiliary insertion, leave the trace indices separate and multiply by DpN ,p0 .
At the positive step the last collapsed vertex has pN = dN , so this factor applies D to the last
output spin of the untwisted row. At the negative step the first collapsed vertex has p0 = −d1 .
Comparing with an untwisted output spin de1 gives the factor D−de ,−d = (JDT J)d ,de . These
                                                                    1   1            1 1
are the two identities in (8.32).
    The product defining GN is invariant under translation by two sites, so [GN , S 2 ] = 0.
Consequently
                    (SGN )∗GN = S −1 GN ,       SGN S −1 GN = S −1 GN SGN .
This proves mutual adjointness, commutativity, normality, and equality of the two norms.
    The matrix C preserves the number of positive spins, has positive diagonal entries, and has
a positive exchange entry on each unlike pair. Thus a step of SGN can hold every complete
pair or can exchange any chosen subset of its unlike pairs, followed by the cyclic shift. Over
N steps, holding all pairs gives the identity permutation. Holding everywhere except for one
chosen pair exchange gives any prescribed adjacent transposition: the shifts before and after
the exchange move that complete pair to any desired cyclic bond. All the corresponding
weights are positive. Adjacent transpositions connect all configurations in a fixed-spin sector,
so SGN is irreducible there. The same argument works with S −1 GN .
    There is also a one-step self-loop in every sector. For a sector with at most N/2 positive
spins, place them on odd sites and put negative spins on every even site. Exchange each unlike
complete pair before applying S; the original configuration is recovered. For a sector with
more than N/2 positive spins, reverse all spins in this construction. For S −1 GN , reverse the
roles of the two parities. These self-loops and irreducibility prove primitivity.
    Perron–Frobenius gives a unique positive ray in each sector and strict modulus inequality
for its remaining eigenvalues. Normality says that the largest Perron eigenvalue over the sectors
equals ΛN . The operator S 2 commutes with SGN and preserves positivity, so uniqueness of the
positive ray makes S 2 Ω a positive multiple of Ω. Since S 2 has finite order, that multiple equals
one. It follows that S −1 GN Ω = S −2 (SGN )Ω = ΛN Ω. Taking GN adjoints and then canceling
the invertible rightmost GN gives the last identity in (8.33). Finally, F is positive on the real
interval under consideration; (8.1) gives F (0) = F (λ) = 1. Thus Φ(λ/4) = Φ(3λ/4) = F (λ/2),
as claimed.

   For later normalization arguments, the preceding lemma also identifies the finite Schatten
moments without a dimension factor. If µ1 , . . . , µ2N are the eigenvalues of TN (+), then for
even M ,
                                      2N
                          M/2 
                                      X                                           1/(N M )     1/N
                                            |µj |M ,   lim Tr (TN (+)TN (−))M/2
                                                             
      Tr (TN (+)TN (−))           =                                                           = ΛN .
                                                       M →∞
                                      j=1
                                                                                      (8.35)
The expression on the left, raised to 1/(N M ), decreases in even M because the unnormalized
ℓM norm of a finite vector decreases with M .
   For the remaining square-medium arguments we write G = GN and TN± = TN (±), keeping
the circumference implicit in the metric.

8.5     The physical scalar divisor
The six-vertex free-energy formula goes back to Lieb and Sutherland [26, 40]. We use the rigor-
ous periodic theorem of Duminil-Copin, Kozlowski, Krachun, Manolescu, and Tikhonovskaia [14,
Theorem 1] at the weights below, and prove the further finite-row normalization needed for
the current estimates.

    Let a∗ = sin(λ/2)/ sin λ. The six-vertex model with paired weights (a∗ , a∗ , 1) has anisotropy

                                         2a2∗ − 1

The following use of the free-energy theorem concerns only periodic six-vertex boxes, not a
random-cluster scaling limit.

Lemma 8.5 (The strip divisor and the periodic pressure). For the ordinary square-lattice
six-vertex model at these weights, the iterated periodic pressure per vertex is

large for each first period gives the stated sequence. Restricting also the second period to even
integers does not change the cylinder Perron limit.
    Here is the identification with F , including its normalization. The harmonic function

    Fix an ordinary periodic k-by-l rectangle. Among its seam spin assignments, choose one

    The number of rows in each sandwich term is fixed while N → ∞. Its products are bounded
in the G norm and the change of divisor is 1 + o(1) per row. This remains true when the
compared terms have different fixed row counts. Their difference therefore changes by an error
tending to zero.

                                                    129
8.6   From tori to the square plane
Proposition 8.7 (Local plane limits and marked ratios). For every fixed finite switch query,
its law in ordinary square-lattice domains converges whenever the distance from the query to
the boundary tends to infinity, uniformly over planar outer cap partitions. These limits define
a unique square-plane switch law. Ordinary and diagonal physical six-vertex tori have the
same limits for every fixed finitely supported spin-insertion ratio, whenever both periods tend
to infinity. The periods need not have bounded aspect ratio. Here a fixed insertion is a finite
product of fixed matrices on physical edges, or a finite linear combination of such products.
The torus trace is unrestricted in total spin; a twist winding around a growing period is not a
fixed insertion in this statement.
Proof. We first work exclusively in the plane. The screen and collar estimates used here are
Propositions 6.15 and 6.17. Only full annuli are needed; they have no bank classes, and the
surrounding primal circuit determines the boundary partition transmitted toward the query.
The bounded collar densities and actual circuit screens of Propositions 5.19, 6.15, and 6.17
give the following uniform statement. For any fixed finite inner query, its conditional law
differs by a quantity tending to zero as the surrounding buffer grows, uniformly over planar
outside partitions. Indeed couple a common positive part on an intermediate annulus. With
a probability bounded below, independent of the two outside partitions, this common part
contains an actual primal circuit. Conditioning toward the inside, the circuit induces the same
wired boundary there. Iterating in separated collars contracts the range of inner probabilities
by a fixed factor, starting from bounded density. Alternating primal and dual circuit screens
also imply that no raw strand meeting a fixed query escapes all large buffers. This proves
existence, uniqueness, self-duality, and lattice symmetry of the planar local limit.
    It is important not to apply a planar boundary estimate directly to the exterior of a
disk in a torus. Instead cut a fundamental chart and remove O(N + M ) seam edges and
vertex identifications. The remaining core is an ordinary plane graph. For any fixed core
edge configuration, restoring the seam changes its component count by at most C(N + M ).
Summing over seam edge states changes its weight relative to the free planar-core law by a
factor between exp(−C(N + M )) and exp(C(N + M )), also after normalization. All constants
depend only on the fixed model parameter.
    In the winding expansion an oriented switch loop of total turn W has factor eiρW/2 , with
a common factor a∗ per physical tile; the local orientation sum is R(λ/2), as verified in
Lemma 8.9 below. A simple contractible loop has turn ±2π, while a simple essential loop
has turn zero, by isotopy to a straight essential cycle on the flat torus. Summing the two
orientations therefore gives loop weights 2 cos(πρ) = d and 2, respectively. To compare with
torus FK, thicken the open primal graph. If its total genus is g, then g ∈ {0, 1}, and its
number of perimeter components is
                                      |A| − |V | + 2k(A) − 2g.
Thus the FK-to-loop discrepancy, apart from fixed factors, is the bounded genus factor q −g
and the essential-loop tilt (2/d)ℓess . Each essential strand has length at least a constant times
min(N, M ); disjointness gives
                                            NM
                              ℓess ≤ C              ≤ C(N + M ).
                                         min(N, M )
Consequently the complete unmarked spin-loop core law satisfies the same pointwise density
comparison with the free planar-core law:

                                  −C(N +M )
                                                  dµtor,core
                                                    N,M
                              e               ≤                ≤ eC(N +M ) .                (8.39)
                                                  dµpl,core
                                                    N,M


                                                    130
    We next obtain a stronger concentration bound under the planar reference law. Let h be
a bounded local switch statistic and write µ(h) for its plane mean. For a tolerance η > 0,
choose a fixed buffer size L so that every conditional mean of an interior translate of h,
given the states outside its buffer, is within η of µ(h). This uses only planar outside states.
Color the translates into a fixed number O(L2 ) of classes having disjoint buffers. In one such
class, conditioning on any earlier values of h is a mixture of full outside configurations for
the next buffer. Its conditional mean is therefore still within η of µ(h). The elementary
bounded-variable exponential estimate, iterated over that class, gives an exponential tail in
the number of its translates. Taking the union over the fixed color classes yields
                                                       
                            µpl,core
                             N,M           h − µ(h) > 3η ≤ Cη e−cη N M                    (8.40)

for the average over translates outside the seam buffer. The omitted strip contains O(L(N +M ))
translates. Its fraction tends to zero whenever both periods grow. By (8.39), the same deviation
probability under the torus law is bounded by Cη exp(C(N + M ) − cη N M ), which tends to
zero without an aspect-ratio restriction. Translation invariance and boundedness then give
convergence of each unmarked local expectation.
    Finally fix finitely many spin insertions. First take a large fixed buffer containing them.
The planar alternating-screen event in that buffer has probability tending to one with its
radius, and by the unmarked local convergence the same is true on the torus. On that event all
raw strands meeting insertions are local. Summing their orientations makes the insertion ratio
a local function of the switches. For m inserted edge matrices, at most m raw loop components
meet insertions, and breaking them at the marked edges leaves at most 2m oriented pieces.
Summing their orientations bounds the modified numerator by a constant depending on the
fixed matrices and m. Their unmarked divisors are products of at most m factors, each
equal to d or 2, and are at least dm > 0. Thus the ratio is bounded uniformly in the torus
periods. This also treats off-diagonal insertions, including those whose total spin change makes
their contraction zero. The complement of the screening event therefore has arbitrarily small
contribution. This proves the assertion for marked ratios and for both slicings.
    Fixed displacements of periods and finitely many changed positive time steps in a balanced
period alter only bounded-width seams; the same proof applies. A long-time cylinder limit
followed by a circumference limit is realized by choosing each time period large enough to
approximate its finite-circumference vacuum coefficient. The comparison above is applied
along this joint sequence, never with one period fixed.

Proof. At fixed N , primitivity identifies the sector-averaged low coefficient, or each of the two

At either physical angle the strip divisor is F (λ/2)N/2 . Thus each row in (8.43) is its normalized
staggered row multiplied by F (λ/2)N/2 /ΛN , which tends to one by Proposition 8.6. Each row
list is fixed while N grows. Proposition 8.3 therefore gives the operator limsup Cλ,L n−1+ρ or
Cλ,L n−1−ρ . This use of the divisor comparison is asymptotic, including when the two high
terms have different row counts.
     For each N , choose one sufficiently large balanced time period that approximates this finite
set of vacuum coefficients: both scalar terms in the high case and both reflected coefficients.
Proposition 8.7 applies along the resulting joint sequence, since (8.32) puts every row twist or
marker on one physical edge. The row counts and all other insertion supports are fixed during
this limit, so the diagrams have finitely supported insertions and both periods tend to infinity.
A twist on each of finitely many such seam edges is covered by that proposition; no insertion
extends around a growing period. It identifies the limits in the statement. In particular the
reflected limits are finite, and they are nonnegative as limits of the norm squares in (8.41); this
does not assert positivity of individual winding summands. Taking the limit in the preceding
Cauchy–Schwarz inequality proves the two bounds.
     For an ordinary tile-axis cut, the untwisted homogeneous row at u = λ/2 is self-adjoint
by (7.26). Within each spin sector its matrix has a positive diagonal and positive transitions
for adjacent unlike-spin exchanges, obtained by two auxiliary exchanges; these connect the
sector, so the row is primitive. Reflection matches the spin coordinates directly. Using its
own Perron divisor and the ordinary Hilbert norm gives the same reflected finite identity and
sector-averaged Cauchy–Schwarz inequality. The fixed-diagram plane limit then gives the last
assertion.

    The finite staggered proposition retains its arbitrary real spectral arguments and arbitrary
vector tests. The plane theorem above concerns only fixed physical-layer diagrams. It allows
finitely many seam matrices on finitely many rows, but it supplies no plane interpretation
for an arbitrary sequence of GN vectors, a nonphysical spectral row, or an insertion winding
around a growing period. The row lists may grow in a later mesh limit; they are fixed during
each circumference limit.
    For geometric current stencils, Proposition A.3 is the separate finite operator identity.
Its common-endpoint construction uses four physical steps in the positive-time direction in
each high term: two marker-sum steps and two unmarked before- or after-twist steps. The
common external tensors and source phase follow from the finite tangency, tangent-lift, and
ice conservation identities. Corollary A.4 combines that identity with Theorem 8.8. Reflection
preserves τ = 1, so its geometric high-stencil phase is unchanged.

A port is high when the immediately preceding boundary gap has the designated wire color.
